## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**

The champion (score 7777.62) is dramatically better than the second-best (9934.29). There's a huge gap between #1 and #2. The champion uses:
- State 0: GoAwayColor (green, color 2) → transitions to state 3 when it sees green (color 7 condition)
- State 1: Attraction → multiple transitions to state 3
- State 2: Stop → transitions to state 0 on black floor
- State 3: Stop → transitions back to state 3 on gray floor

The key insight: The mission wants robots to be **stationary before the switch** and **moving after the switch**. The green light signals the switch. So the strategy should be:
1. Stay stopped before the green light appears
2. Move when you see the green light

The champion has a complex structure. Let me understand the flow:
- State 0 (GoAwayColor green): moves away from green, transitions to state 3 when sees green (0.79 prob)
- State 3 (Stop): transitions to state 3 (itself? No - destination 3 means the 4th state among others... wait)

- s2=Explore, stays

Machine 14: 2-state exploration of different color detection
- s0=Stop, see green → s1 (p=0.85), also random → s1 (p=0.0003) [in case green not visible]
Wait, can't have two transitions of same type from one state? Actually conditions must be different types. C=7 (see color) and C=5 (random) are different, so two transitions are fine.

Machine 14 (2-state with two transition types):
- s0=Stop, 2 transitions: C=7 see green p=0.9 → s1, C=5 random p=0.0002 → s1
- s1=Explore rwm=50, 1 transition: tiny random back

OK let me just write them all out cleanly now:

---

**Machine 1** (Champion exact copy):
--nstates 4 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 0 --clr0 2 --n0 1 --n0x0 3 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.7943 --l0x0 2 --s1 4 --att1 1.8228 --cle1 4 --n1 4 --n1x0 3 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.3125 --n1x1 3 --c1x1 2 --p1x1 0.8416 --n1x2 3 --c1x2 4 --p1x2 1 --w1x2 0.2335 --n1x3 3 --c1x3 2 --p1x3 0.5406 --s2 1 --cle2 4 --n2 1 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 0 --p2x0 0.9411 --s3 1 --cle3 5 --n3 1 --n3x0 3 --c3x0 2 --p3x0 0.4396

**Machine 2** (Champion tweak: p0x0=0.9):
Same but p0x0 0.9

**Machine 3** (Champion tweak: att1=2.2, p0x0=0.85):
Same but att1 2.2, p0x0 0.85

Same but p3x0 0.55

**Machine 5** (2-state simple):
--nstates 2 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --s1 0 --rwm1 50 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001

**Machine 6** (2-state, p=0.95):
--nstates 2 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.95 --l0x0 2 --s1 0 --rwm1 30 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001

**Machine 7** (2-state Stop→GoToColor green):
--nstates 2 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 2 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001

**Machine 8** (3-state Stop→Explore→GoToColor):
--nstates 3 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --s1 0 --rwm1 50 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 7 --p1x0 0.8 --l1x0 2 --s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 6 --n2 1 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 5 --p2x0 0.001

Here: s0=Stop→(see green)→s1(Explore), s1=Explore→(see green)→s2(GoToColor green), s2=GoToColor→tiny random→s0(Stop) [bad, but p=0.001 so rare]

Wait: from state 2 (GoToColor), others=[0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1. D=0 with p=0.001 → rarely goes back to Stop. That's acceptable.

**Machine 9** (3-state Stop with two triggers):
--nstates 3 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.0003 --s1 0 --rwm1 50 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001 --s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 6 --n2 1 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 5 --p2x0 0.001

Wait I mixed up the states. Let me redo:
- s0=Stop: see green(C=7,l=2)→D=0(state1), random(C=5,p=0.0003)→D=0(state1)... but both targets are D=0. That's fine, two different conditions can have same destination.

Actually both transitions go to state 1 (explore). From state 0, others=[1,2]. D=0→state1. Both n0x0 and n0x1 have D=0→state1. That's fine.

But wait, I have --n0 2 with n0x0 D=0 and n0x1 D=0 both going to state 1. That's OK, different conditions, same destination.

Let me restructure: s0=Stop→explore(green,p=0.9) or random(p=0.0003), s1=Explore→GoToColor(green seen, p=0.8), s2=GoToColor→random tiny back

**Machine 10** (4-state):
--nstates 4 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --n0x1 1 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.0002 --s1 0 --rwm1 50 --cle1 5 --n1 2 --

State 0 (GoAwayColor green): n0=1, n0x0 D=2 → state3(Stop), C=7 see green(2), p=0.7943
State 1 (Attraction): n1=4, all D=2 → state3(Stop), conditions: random, gray floor, neighbors<1, gray floor
State 2 (Stop): n2=1, n2x0 D=0 → state0(GoAwayColor), C=0 black floor, p=0.9411
State 3 (Stop): n3=1, n3x0 D=2 → state2(Stop... wait D=2 from state3: others=[0,1,2], D=2→state2), C=2 gray floor, p=0.4396

So state 3 on gray floor → state 2 (Stop). State 2 on black floor (p=0.9411) → state 0 (GoAwayColor). Since floor is gray, state 2 rarely transitions to state 0. State 3 on gray floor → state 2 (also Stop). This seems like two Stop states that cycle between each other.

The key flow: Start in state 0 (GoAwayColor = moving). See green → state 3 (Stop). State 3 on gray floor (very likely since floor is gray) → state 2 (Stop). State 2 on black floor (very unlikely) → state 0. So after green appears: Stop → Stop → rarely GoAwayColor.

Before switch: GoAwayColor (moving) = good score (no penalty for moving before switch).
After switch: Stop = good score (no penalty for stopping after switch, wait - after switch penalty is for MOVING).

Wait, I need to re-read again: "From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving." So after switch, moving = bad, stationary = good. And "Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary." Before switch, stationary = bad, moving = good.

So:
- Before switch: MOVE (good) or STOP (bad, +1 per robot per step)
- After switch: STOP (good) or MOVE (bad, +1 per robot per step)

Champion: GoAwayColor before switch → moving → good (no penalty). After green detected → Stop → good (no penalty). Makes perfect sense! Score ~7482 is the residual from the delay in detecting green.

**New machines should**: Start MOVING, detect green → STOP.

FSM 12: 
- s0=Explore(rwm=50), n0=2: C=7(l=2,p=0.9)→D=0(s1=Stop), C=5(p=0.0001)→D=0(s1=Stop) [both go to Stop]
  Wait, can two transitions have same destination? Yes, I think so. Both go D=0→s1.
  Actually no, from s0, D=0→s1 and there's only one index for D. Both n0x0 and n0x1 can have D=0.
- s1=Stop, n1=1: C=5(p=0.001)→D=0(s0=Explore)
- s2=GoAwayColor(2), n2=1: C=7(l=2,p=0.9)→D=1(s1=Stop) [from s2, others=[0,1], D=1→s1]

Hmm, but how does anyone get to s2? Let me restructure:
- s0=Explore(rwm=50), n0=1: C=7(l=2,p=0.9)→D=1(s2=Stop)
- s1=GoAwayColor(2), n1=1: C=7(l=2,p=0.9)→D=1(s2=Stop) [from s1, others=[0,2], D=1→s2]
- s2=Stop, n2=1: C=5(p=0.001)→D=0(s0=Explore) [from s2, others=[0,1], D=0→s0]

But s1=GoAway is never reached from s0 or s2. That's wasteful. Let me use a 2-state or make s1 reachable.

Actually let me just do 2-state with two transition types:
**FSM 12**: 2-state Explore→Stop with two triggers:
- s0=Explore(rwm=50), n0=2: C=7(l=2,p=0.9)→D=0(s1=Stop), C=5(p=0.0002)→D=0(s1=Stop)
  But two transitions with D=0 both... is that allowed? Let me check: --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 ... --n0x1 0 --c0x1 5 ... Both D=0→s1. Should be valid (different condition types).
- s1=Stop, n1=1: C=5(p=0.001)→D=0(s0=Explore)

Hmm but two transitions to same destination - is that useful? The random trigger (C=5, p=0.0002) would cause very rare transitions to Stop even without green. That's a bad idea (we want to move before switch). Skip this, use a different approach.

**FSM 12**: 3-state with neighbor sensing:
- s0=Explore(rwm=50), n0=2: C=7(l=2,p=0.9)→D=1(s2=Stop), C=4(neighbors<1,p=1,w=5)→D=0(s1=Attraction) [spread out if isolated]
  Wait C=4 is "neighbors BELOW threshold". --p is INTEGER neighbor count. p=1 means threshold=1 neighbor (below 1 = isolated). This would trigger when robot has 0 neighbors. That might be useful for spreading.
  Actually we want to MOVE before switch. Exploration already moves. Let me not complicate.

Let me simplify FSM 12 to a straightforward 3-state:
- s0=Explore(rwm=50), n0=1: C=7(l=2,p=0.9)→D=1(s2=Stop)
- s1=Attraction(att=2.0), n1=1: C=7(l=2,p=0.9)→D=1(s2=Stop) [from s1, others=[0,2], D=1→s2]
- s2=Stop, n2=1: C=5(p=0.001)→D=0(s0=Explore) [from s2, others=[0,1], D=0→s0]

And how does s1 (Attraction) get reached? It doesn't from s0 or s2. This is wasteful. Let me make s0→s1 possible:
- s

[essai 2/2]
## Reasoning

The correct strategy is: MOVE before the green wall appears, STOP after detecting it. The champion (GoAwayColor→Stop on green) confirms this. I'll copy the champion, make several close tweaks, and add clean 2-state and 3-state move→stop-on-green machines.

## Reasoning

The champion (6784.88) is a 3-state machine: GoAwayColor(green) with two exits (see green→Stop, rare random→Explore), Stop with tiny random→GoAwayColor, Explore→see green→Stop. The key insight is GoAwayColor(green) moves robots before the switch (good), and the rare random fallback to Explore keeps robots moving if they drift to Stop early. I'll exploit this topology heavily with parameter tweaks and explore slight structural variants.

## Reasoning

The champion (5143.19) uses p0x1=0.001 for the random fallback from GoAwayColor to Explore, while the 5e-04 variants score ~5850-5890. The key difference is the random fallback probability: 0.001 is better than 5e-04. I'll exploit the champion topology by sweeping p0x1 around 0.001, trying 0.002, 0.003, 0.0015, and also try adding a third transition to s0 (neighbor-based) as seen in rank 2. I'll also try variants where s2 has an additional green-detection transition back to s1 for faster stopping after switch.

## Reasoning

The champion (5029.77) uses p0x1=0.0015 for the random fallback. The second best (5034.77) uses 0.002. The sweet spot is around 0.0015-0.002. Scores 5-7 all use 0.001 and get ~5168. The pattern is clear: p0x1 around 0.0015-0.002 is optimal. I'll fine-tune around this range (0.0012, 0.0015, 0.0017, 0.002, 0.0025) and also try combining the champion's p0x1 with the rank-3 variant (s2 with extra random transition) and rank-4 (p0x0=0.95).

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
